That's actually the main thing. I just feel so keyed up all the time now. Like I said, I'm lying awake at night, my mind is just racing, worrying about every little decision I made on the floor that day or what the manager is going to say tomorrow. It's just this constant, nervous energy that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Is this stress starting to interfere with how well you do your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. My work record has actually taken a hit lately. I've missed some internal deadlines, and... well, I've been pulling back from meetings I'm expected to attend at the plant. \n\nAnd honestly, it's affecting things at home, too. My husband and I are arguing a lot more, and I'm just not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: If this conflict with the new manager were resolved, do you think your anxiety would go away?\n\nPatient: I think it would, yeah. I mean, this situation hasn't really ended yet, so it's hard to say for absolutely sure. I still have to report to him every shift and deal with the procedures he changed on the floor. I haven't had any period where the conflict was actually resolved and these problems just kept going for months on their own, you know? So... yes, if things were fixed, I really believe the anxiety would go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your reaction to this situation at work has been much stronger than most people would have in the same situation?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I mean... maybe? It's hard to judge. I know I'm really struggling, and it feels like a lot to deal with, but I haven't really compared myself to how anyone else is handling it. I just know it's affecting me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you been acting out or behaving in ways that are out of character for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't been doing anything reckless or... acting out, like you said. I'm just stressed and having trouble keeping up with things, but I'm not doing anything crazy or completely out of character.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of distress about this situation is more intense than the situation would usually warrant?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure how to answer that. I mean, it feels warranted to me because of how much it's impacting my day-to-day life, but I don't know if someone else would look at it and think I'm overreacting. I just know that the distress is very real for me.\n\nDoctor: Because of this stress at work, have you had to miss work or been unable to do important parts of your job?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's definitely affecting my job. My work record has taken a bit of a hit lately. I've missed some internal deadlines, which isn't like me, and I've been pulling back from meetings I'm expected to attend. I just can't seem to face them right now. And it's not just at work... at home, my husband and I are arguing more than usual, and I'm just not keeping up with our normal evening routines the way I used to. Everything just feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your emotional reaction to what’s happening at work has been stronger than most people would have in the same situation?\n\nPatient: I guess that's kind of what you just asked, isn't it? I really don't know how to compare my reaction to what most people would do. I just know that for me, it feels like a lot, and I'm struggling to handle it. I couldn't tell you if someone else in my exact job would be this upset.\n\nDoctor: Is the conflict with your new manager still ongoing right now?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's definitely still ongoing. I mean, I still have to report to him every day, and I'm still dealing with all the new procedures he put in place. The situation hasn't ended at all, so it's not like the problem was resolved and I just kept being upset about it for months afterward. I'm still right in the middle of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So... yes, if things were fixed, I really believe the anxiety would go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your reaction to this situation at work has been much stronger than most people would have in the same situation?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I mean... maybe? It's hard to judge. I know I'm really struggling, and it feels like a lot to deal with, but I haven't really compared myself to how anyone else is handling it. I just know it's affecting me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you been acting out or behaving in ways that are out of character for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't been doing anything reckless or... acting out, like you said. I'm just stressed and having trouble keeping up with things, but I'm not doing anything crazy or completely out of character.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of distress about this situation is more intense than the situation would usually warrant?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure how to answer that. I mean, it feels warranted to me because of how much it's impacting my day-to-day life, but I don't know if someone else would look at it and think I'm overreacting. I just know that the distress is very real for me.\n\nDoctor: Because of this stress at work, have you had to miss work or been unable to do important parts of your job?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's definitely affecting my job. My work record has taken a bit of a hit lately. I've missed some internal deadlines, which isn't like me, and I've been pulling back from meetings I'm expected to attend. I just can't seem to face them right now. And it's not just at work... at home, my husband and I are arguing more than usual, and I'm just not keeping up with our normal evening routines the way I used to. Everything just feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your emotional reaction to what’s happening at work has been stronger than most people would have in the same situation?\n\nPatient: I guess that's kind of what you just asked, isn't it? I really don't know how to compare my reaction to what most people would do. I just know that for me, it feels like a lot, and I'm struggling to handle it. I couldn't tell you if someone else in my exact job would be this upset.\n\nDoctor: Is the conflict with your new manager still ongoing right now?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's definitely still ongoing. I mean, I still have to report to him every day, and I'm still dealing with all the new procedures he put in place. The situation hasn't ended at all, so it's not like the problem was resolved and I just kept being upset about it for months afterward. I'm still right in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your distress about the situation at work is out of proportion to what most people in your position would feel?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really couldn't tell you. It's hard to judge what would be out of proportion for somebody else. I just know that it's really getting to me, but... yeah, I don't know how another inspector would handle the exact same thing. I don't have a good way to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Did your anxiety and stress start within three months after the new operations manager began changing your procedures?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely did. I was doing just fine before the new operations manager came in. The trouble really started building within just a few weeks of him changing our procedures. I'd say by the second month, I... well, I knew my reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday.\n\nDoctor: Compared with what most people in your position would feel, do you think your reaction to this situation has been stronger than expected?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned just a minute ago, I really don't know. I guess I don't know what the expected reaction would even be. I just know it's been really hard on me, but... yeah, I can't really compare it to what someone else would feel in my shoes.\n\nDoctor: Has your stress about this situation felt clearly stronger than the situation itself would usually call for?\n\nPatient: I... I feel like you're asking me the same question again. Like I've been saying, I honestly don't know what this kind of situation usually calls for. I just know that it's overwhelming for me right now. I really can't say if my stress is stronger than it should be... I just know it's a lot to deal with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean... maybe? It's hard to judge. I know I'm really struggling, and it feels like a lot to deal with, but I haven't really compared myself to how anyone else is handling it. I just know it's affecting me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you been acting out or behaving in ways that are out of character for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't been doing anything reckless or... acting out, like you said. I'm just stressed and having trouble keeping up with things, but I'm not doing anything crazy or completely out of character.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of distress about this situation is more intense than the situation would usually warrant?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure how to answer that. I mean, it feels warranted to me because of how much it's impacting my day-to-day life, but I don't know if someone else would look at it and think I'm overreacting. I just know that the distress is very real for me.\n\nDoctor: Because of this stress at work, have you had to miss work or been unable to do important parts of your job?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's definitely affecting my job. My work record has taken a bit of a hit lately. I've missed some internal deadlines, which isn't like me, and I've been pulling back from meetings I'm expected to attend. I just can't seem to face them right now. And it's not just at work... at home, my husband and I are arguing more than usual, and I'm just not keeping up with our normal evening routines the way I used to. Everything just feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your emotional reaction to what’s happening at work has been stronger than most people would have in the same situation?\n\nPatient: I guess that's kind of what you just asked, isn't it? I really don't know how to compare my reaction to what most people would do. I just know that for me, it feels like a lot, and I'm struggling to handle it. I couldn't tell you if someone else in my exact job would be this upset.\n\nDoctor: Is the conflict with your new manager still ongoing right now?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's definitely still ongoing. I mean, I still have to report to him every day, and I'm still dealing with all the new procedures he put in place. The situation hasn't ended at all, so it's not like the problem was resolved and I just kept being upset about it for months afterward. I'm still right in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your distress about the situation at work is out of proportion to what most people in your position would feel?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really couldn't tell you. It's hard to judge what would be out of proportion for somebody else. I just know that it's really getting to me, but... yeah, I don't know how another inspector would handle the exact same thing. I don't have a good way to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Did your anxiety and stress start within three months after the new operations manager began changing your procedures?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely did. I was doing just fine before the new operations manager came in. The trouble really started building within just a few weeks of him changing our procedures. I'd say by the second month, I... well, I knew my reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday.\n\nDoctor: Compared with what most people in your position would feel, do you think your reaction to this situation has been stronger than expected?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned just a minute ago, I really don't know. I guess I don't know what the expected reaction would even be. I just know it's been really hard on me, but... yeah, I can't really compare it to what someone else would feel in my shoes.\n\nDoctor: Has your stress about this situation felt clearly stronger than the situation itself would usually call for?\n\nPatient: I... I feel like you're asking me the same question again. Like I've been saying, I honestly don't know what this kind of situation usually calls for. I just know that it's overwhelming for me right now. I really can't say if my stress is stronger than it should be... I just know it's a lot to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone you trust told you that your reaction to this situation seems stronger than they would expect?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I haven't really talked about it with anyone else, to be honest. I just go to work, do my inspecting, and come home. So... no one's really said anything like that to me.\n\nDoctor: Aside from its impact on your job, has this work situation caused you marked emotional distress?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, it's definitely stressful and it's on my mind a lot, but... I wouldn't call it severe emotional distress outside of the job itself. Like I said, I just go in, do my inspecting, and come home. It's an overwhelming situation to deal with, but I'm not breaking down crying at home or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don't have a good way to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Did your anxiety and stress start within three months after the new operations manager began changing your procedures?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely did. I was doing just fine before the new operations manager came in. The trouble really started building within just a few weeks of him changing our procedures. I'd say by the second month, I... well, I knew my reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday.\n\nDoctor: Compared with what most people in your position would feel, do you think your reaction to this situation has been stronger than expected?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned just a minute ago, I really don't know. I just know it's been really hard on me, but... yeah, I can't really compare it to what someone else would feel in my shoes.\n\nDoctor: Has your stress about this situation felt clearly stronger than the situation itself would usually call for?\n\nPatient: I... I feel like you're asking me the same question again. Like I've been saying, I honestly don't know what this kind of situation usually calls for. I just know that it's overwhelming for me right now. I really can't say if my stress is stronger than it should be... I just know it's a lot to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone you trust told you that your reaction to this situation seems stronger than they would expect?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I haven't really talked about it with anyone else, to be honest. I just go to work, do my inspecting, and come home. So... no one's really said anything like that to me.\n\nDoctor: Aside from its impact on your job, has this work situation caused you marked emotional distress?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, it's definitely stressful and it's on my mind a lot, but... I wouldn't call it severe emotional distress outside of the job itself. Like I said, I just go in, do my inspecting, and come home. It's an overwhelming situation to deal with, but I'm not breaking down crying at home or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Because of this situation at work, has your functioning at home been impaired?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it has. At home, my husband and I have been arguing a lot more lately. And I'm just... I'm not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. It's affecting work, too. My record there has taken a hit because I've been missing internal deadlines, and I've kind of pulled back from meetings I'm supposed to be at. So, yes, it's definitely bleeding into everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just know it's been really hard on me, but... yeah, I can't really compare it to what someone else would feel in my shoes.\n\nDoctor: Has your stress about this situation felt clearly stronger than the situation itself would usually call for?\n\nPatient: I... I just know it's a lot to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone you trust told you that your reaction to this situation seems stronger than they would expect?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I haven't really talked about it with anyone else, to be honest. I just go to work, do my inspecting, and come home. So... no one's really said anything like that to me.\n\nDoctor: Aside from its impact on your job, has this work situation caused you marked emotional distress?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, it's definitely stressful and it's on my mind a lot, but... I wouldn't call it severe emotional distress outside of the job itself. Like I said, I just go in, do my inspecting, and come home. It's an overwhelming situation to deal with, but I'm not breaking down crying at home or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Because of this situation at work, has your functioning at home been impaired?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it has. At home, my husband and I have been arguing a lot more lately. And I'm just... I'm not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. It's affecting work, too. My record there has taken a hit because I've been missing internal deadlines, and I've kind of pulled back from meetings I'm supposed to be at. So, yes, it's definitely bleeding into everything.\n\nDoctor: Has this situation at work caused you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: I think I just answered that, but... no, not really. It's stressful, sure, and it's definitely messing with my routines and my marriage, but I wouldn't say I'm in marked emotional distress. I'm just stressed out and falling behind."
